## Page 1


CATEGORY O FOR TRUNCATED SHIFTED YANGIANS
AND THE BI-INFINITE BOTT-SAMELSON VARIETY
JOEL KAMNITZER, ANTOINE LABELLE, ALEXIS LEROUX-LAPIERRE, TH´EO PINET,
AND ALEX WEEKES
Abstract. In this paper, we study the category O of representations of shifted Yangians
associated to a simply-laced simple Lie algebra g over C. In particular, we prove that the
(complexified) Grothendieck ring of this category is isomorphic to the Cox ring of the open
bi-infinite Bott–Samelson variety, which is a pro-variety we construct from Bott–Samelson
varieties for alternating heaps. Using work of Francone–Leclerc, we prove a conjecture
of Hernandez–Zhang by identifying the above Grothendieck ring with a cluster algebra
defined by Geiss–Hernandez–Leclerc. Our methods also yield an action of the Langlands
dual group G∨on this Grothendieck ring, and show that the shifted coproducts defined
in work of the first and fifth authors with collaborators give rise to coproducts for trun-
cated shifted Yangians. This machinery then allows us to prove further conjectures of
Frenkel–Hernandez and Geiss–Hernandez–Leclerc on extended QQ-systems, and to ob-
tain a generalization of a duality defined by Hernandez–Leclerc.

J. KAMNITZER, A. LABELLE, A. LEROUX-LAPIERRE, T. PINET, AND A. WEEKES
1. Introduction
1.1. Shifted quantum groups and Grothendieck rings. Fix a simply-laced simple Lie
algebra g over C with Dynkin diagram I. In his seminal paper [Dri85], Drinfeld introduced
the Yangian Y (g), an associative algebra deforming the universal enveloping algebra of the
current algebra g[t]. A key property of Y (g) is the existence of a coproduct
∆: Y (g) →Y (g) ⊗Y (g),
which endows the category C of finite-dimensional Y (g)-modules with a monoidal structure,
and thus makes the associated Grothendieck group K0(C ) into a ring. The ring K0(C ), and
its trigonometric analogue (coming from representations of quantum affine algebras), have
been extensively studied in recent years (see, e.g., [CP90; Fuj+22; Kas+24]). In particular, a
major breakthrough occurred when Hernandez–Leclerc [HL10] proposed a geometric inter-
pretation of the intricate relations governing the Grothendieck ring K0(C ) in terms of Lie-
theoretic varieties and their cluster structures. In this paper, we extend this interpretation
by relating the category O of representations of shifted Yangians with a pro-variety we call
the bi-infinite Bott–Samelson variety and a cluster algebra introduced in [GHL24].
The shifted Yangians Yµ(g) are associative algebras that depend on a coweight µ ∈P ∨of
g. They were defined for g = gln and µ dominant by Brundan–Kleshchev [BK06], and were
generalized to arbitrary g and µ by the first and fifth authors and collaborators in [BFN19;
Fin+18]. Moreover, as shown in [Fin+18], there is, for any µ1, µ2 ∈P ∨, a shifted coproduct
(1)
∆µ1,µ2 : Yµ1+µ2(g) →Yµ1(g) ⊗Yµ2(g)
that recovers the coproduct for Y (g) = Y0(g) when µ1 = µ2 = 0.
In [HZ24], Hernandez–Zhang began a systematic study of the category O associated to
Yµ(g), which we denote by Oµ (see Definition 4.1 for details). By their work, simple objects
of Oµ are characterized using their highest ℓ-weights, which are I-tuples of rational functions
ψ = (ψi(u))i∈I whose expansion at u = ∞describes the action of the loop-Cartan subalgebra
of Yµ(g) on a highest ℓ-weight vector. Moreover, Hernandez–Zhang defined a generalization
of Knight’s ℓ-character map [Kni95]
(2)
χℓ: K0(Oµ) →Eℓ
which sends the class of a module V of Oµ to a generating series that records the dimensions
of the weight spaces of V for the action of the loop Cartan subalgebra of Yµ(g). By [HZ24,
Theorem 3.14], this map gives an injective morphism of rings
χℓ: K0(L
µ∈P ∨Oµ) →Eℓ,
where the ring structure for the left-hand side comes from the shifted coproducts (1) (and
where the multiplication for Eℓis similar to the usual product of generating series).
We call the category
Osh := L
µ∈P ∨Oµ
the category O for shifted Yangians. By the above, its Grothendieck group K0(Osh) has the
structure of a commutative and associative ring.
Remark. The above discussion has a trigonometric analogue in terms of shifted quantum
affine algebras [FT19; Her23]. Although formulated differently, this alternative framework

TRUNCATED SHIFTED YANGIANS AND THE BI-INFINITE BOTT-SAMELSON VARIETY
3
is (at least for our purposes) interchangeable with the present setting via the equivalences of
“truncated categories” given in [VV25, Corollary 1.2.1] (see also [DK25] and [GT16]). More
precisely, combining these equivalences with [HZ25, Theorem 5.4] and [Kam+19b, Corollary
5.22] gives a ring isomorphism between K0(Osh) and its trigonometric counterpart (studied
for example in [Her23; GHL24]) that is compatible with ℓ-characters on both sides and maps
simple classes to simple classes. Accordingly, we freely pass between the trigonometric and
rational (i.e. shifted Yangian) frameworks throughout the paper.
We will be interested in a particular subcategory of Osh. Fix a bipartition on the Dynkin
diagram I = I0 ⊔I1 into even and odd vertices and let
I ×2 Z = {(i, a) ∈I × Z | i and a have the same parity}.
We say that a highest ℓ-weight ψ is integral if the zeros and poles of each rational function
ψi(u) are integers of the same parity as i. Denote by OZ
sh the full subcategory of Osh whose
objects have finite length and for which each simple constituent L(ψ) has an integral highest
ℓ-weight ψ. In Section 5.3, we prove that this subcategory is closed under tensor products
and that the study of the ring K0(Osh) essentially reduces to the study of K0(OZ
sh).
The main motivating question of our current work is:

sh)?

1.9. Hernandez–Leclerc’s duality. Another consequence of our results concerns a mor-
phism of Grothendieck rings defined in [HL16] in the context of monoidal categorifications
of cluster algebras arising from the representation theory of Borel subalgebras of quantum

Theorem 14. The morphism D sends classes of simple objects to classes of simple objects.
In particular, it sends the class of the positive prefundamental module L(Ψi,a) to the class of
the negative prefundamental module L(Ψ−1
i,−a) for all (i, a) ∈I ×2 Z. Moreover, the map D

1.10. Further research directions. Throughout this paper, we make progress towards
Geiss–Hernandez–Leclerc’s conjecture that OZ
sh is a monoidal categorification of the cluster
algebra A . Indeed, in Theorem 8.28, we prove that all chamber modules, that is all the
modules corresponding to cluster variables in the initial seeds described in [GHL24], are
real (i.e. of simple tensor square) and prime (i.e. they cannot be factorized non-trivially as
tensor products in OZ
sh). Furthermore, we highlight in Section 6.7 that the graded structure
on KLRW algebras should give rise to a “natural” t-deformation of K0(OZ
sh). We argue here
that this deformation, along with the R-matrices we construct in Section 5.2 (generalizing
results of [HZ24]), should help prove Geiss–Hernandez–Leclerc’s conjecture using a method
analogous to that used in [Kan+18; CW19]. We also think that the deformation of K0(OZ
sh)
coming from KLRW algebras should be compatible with the limit of the standard Poisson
structure on Bott–Samelson varieties studied in [EL21], as well as with Paganelli’s quantum
cluster algebra At,w0 [Pag26], and Fujita–Hernandez’s monoidal Jantzen filtrations [FH26].
By carefully tracing through the different definitions, it is not hard to see that both Z∞
and the Cox ring R are defined over any field k of characteristic 0. Our proofs actually show
that Ωis an isomorphism of k-algebras from R to k ⊗Z K0(Osh). It would be interesting
to determine if this works over fields of positive characteristic as well.

1.12. Outline of the paper. Section 2 introduces Nakajima’s monomial crystal and the
product monomial crystal. We establish combinatorial criteria that will be used throughout
the paper in Theorem 2.12 and we characterize maximally singular sets of parameters in
Theorem 2.15. A brief discussion concerning the non-integral case appears in Section 2.4.


## Page 13


TRUNCATED SHIFTED YANGIANS AND THE BI-INFINITE BOTT-SAMELSON VARIETY
13
Section 3 defines shifted Yangians, shifted coproducts, truncated shifted Yangians together
with all the necessary technical tools related to those algebras. Section 4 studies modules
over shifted Yangians. There, we recall the definition of the category Osh with the truncated
categories Oλ
µ(R). We give a direct sum decomposition for Osh in Theorem 4.22 and define
chamber modules in Section 4.4. We also introduce GT-characters in Section 4.5 which are
one of the main tools used to study the G∨-action on KC(Osh). Section 5 starts by giving
combinatorial criteria for identifying highest ℓ-weight objects which, when combined with
Theorem 5.2, produce families of R-matrices. We then prove generic simplicity results for
tensor products of simple objects in Theorem 5.23 and show in Section 5.4 that coproducts
descend to truncations.
The Interlude marks an important separation in the paper, where we switch the roles of
G and G∨and restrict ourselves to the integral part of Osh.
Section 6 recalls KLR algebras, KLRW algebras and parity KLRW algebras. A yoga of
shuffle products for parity KLRW algebra modules is developed in Section 6.6 and Theorem
6.33 compares it with the product of GT-characters. Section 7 explains how to glue the G-
action on each KC(Oλ
sh(R)) into a G-action on KC(Osh). Section 8 applies previous results
to the proof of certain relations in K0(Osh). Theorem 8.6 studies relations coming from the
base affine space while Theorem 8.13 shows that isoclasses of chamber modules satisfy the
extended QQ-system. A discussion about real and prime objects can be found in Section
8.5. Section 9 switches gears and tackles the geometry of Bott–Samelson varieties. Section
10 introduces the bi-infinite Bott–Samelson variety Z∞and the Cox ring R of the open
cell Z◦
∞⊆Z∞, for which a presentation is given in Corollary 10.20. Finally, Section 10.2
studies spaces of sections of the line bundles Lλ,R and proves they have the same characters
as the product monomial crystals B(λ, R). Section 11 relates the geometry to the category
Osh. This culminates in a proof that KC(Osh) is isomorphic to R, which can be found in
Theorems 11.2, 11.5 and 11.7. After this, Section 11.5 identifies notable full subcategories
Osh(ξ, ξ′) ⊆Osh categorifying the coordinate rings C[ bZ◦
ξ,ξ′]. Special cases recover C[N−\G]
and C[G]. Corollary 11.21 gives an alternative presentation of K0(Osh) in type A. Section
12 studies an involution of the scheme bZ◦
∞(cf. Theorem 12.13) and transports it to KC(Osh)
via the isomorphism Ω. We show in Theorem 12.17 that the resulting involution of KC(Osh)
preserves classes of simple objects and prove in Section 12.5 that this involution restricts to
a duality studied previously by Hernandez–Leclerc.
The article concludes with two appendices. Appendix A gives the necessary tool-kit for
dealing with the pro-varieties of Section 10. Appendix B studies how truncated categories
behave under inclusions of Dynkin diagrams. This leads to a notion of inflation for modules
over shifted Yangians and proves a conjecture made by the fourth author in [Pin25]. Results
of this appendix are also used in Section 8.4 in order to give a conjectural categorification
of the extended QQ-system via an exact sequence happening in the principal block for sl2.

of multisets.

Let X be a set. We will write ZX for the collection of functions from X to Z. We will
represent such functions as formal linear combinations of the form P
x∈X ax[x] for ax ∈Z.
Here, [x] ∈ZX denotes the delta function at x ∈X, i.e. [x](x) = 1 and [x](y) = 0 for all
y ̸= x. This function will also be denoted by δx.
Throughout, I will denote a simply-laced Dynkin diagram. For i, j ∈I, write i ∼j if i
is connected to j. Fix a decomposition I = I0 ⊔I1 into even and odd vertices which makes
I into a bipartite graph (i.e. no edge has vertices of the same parity). We will denote by
i ∈Z/2Z the parity of i ∈I. We will often use the two sets
I ×2 Z := {(i, a) ∈I × Z : a −i ∈2Z},
I ×op
2 Z := {(i, b) ∈I × Z : b −i ∈2Z + 1}.
Accordingly, we have i + 2Z = {a ∈Z | (i, a) ∈I ×2 Z}. We endow I ×2 Z with the partial
order ≤generated by the relations (i, a) ≤(i, a + 2) and (i, a) ≤(j, a + 1) whenever i ∼j.
Equivalently, we have (i, a) ≤(j, b) if and only if a + d(i, j) ≤b, where d(i, j) denotes the
distance in I between the vertices i and j. We also endow I ×op
2 Z with the partial order
defined in the same way. In Section 6.5, we will be using a total order on I ×2 Z which
should not be confused with the previously defined partial order. It will depend on the
extra data of a total order on I such that even vertices are smaller than odd vertices.
1.13.1. The Grothendieck group. Let C be an abelian category. We denote its Grothendieck
group by K0(C). When C is of finite-length, the abelian group K0(C) has a basis given by
isoclasses of simple objects. If C also has a tensor product ⊗: C ×C →C, the Grothendieck
group K0(C) becomes a ring by defining [M1] · [M2] := [M1 ⊗M2]. The complexification of
K0(C) is denoted by KC(C) := C ⊗Z K0(C).

sociated to the simply-laced Dynkin diagram I. Given a complex algebraic group G whose
Lie algebra is g, we fix B a choice of Borel subgroup of G together with T ⊆B a maximal
torus. We denote by N the unipotent radical of B. We let B−be the opposite Borel
subgroup with respect to B and N−be its unipotent radical. The complex Lie algebras of
the groups T, N, N−, B, B−are denoted by h, n, n−, b, b−respectively.

Hence, weights of V are all weights of V (λ) and Theorem 2.4 implies the result.

algebra (g∨)⊕k where k is the number of distinct integrality classes in R (cf. [Kam+19a]).

3. Truncated shifted Yangians
This section recalls the definition of truncated shifted Yangians as images of remarkable
homomorphisms [Ger+05] and gives the important properties of the shifted coproducts ∆µ,ν
of [Fin+18]. Our exposition closely follows that of [Fin+18; HZ24; Kam+24].
3.1. Shifted Yangians. As mentioned in the introduction, dominantly shifted Yangians
of type A were first introduced in [BK06] as subalgebras of Drinfeld’s Yangian Y (gln). This
definition was then extended to all simple types in [Kam+14], and, using “Cartan doubled
Yangians”, to arbitrary (i.e. not necessarily dominant) shifts in [BFN19; Fin+18].


## Page 23

23
Definition 3.1 ([BFN19; Fin+18]). The Cartan doubled Yangian Y∞(of g) is the algebra
generated by elements ei,q, fi,q, hi,p for i ∈I, q ∈Z>0 and p ∈Z, with defining relations2
[hi,p, hj,p′] = 0,
(26)

(27)
[hi,p+1, ej,q] −[hi,p, ej,q+1] = cij(hi,pej,q + ej,qhi,p),
(28)
[hi,p+1, fj,q] −[hi,p, fj,q+1] = −cij(hi,pfj,q + fj,qhi,p),
(29)
[ei,q+1, ej,q′] −[ei,q, ej,q′+1] = cij(ei,qej,q′ + ej,q′ei,q),
(30)
[fi,q+1, fj,q′] −[fi,q, fj,q′+1] = −cij(fi,qfj,q′ + fj,q′fi,q),
(31)
i ̸= j, N = 1 −cij =⇒sym[ei,q1, [ei,q2, · · · [ei,qN , ej,q′] · · · ]] = 0,
(32)
i ̸= j, N = 1 −cij =⇒sym[fi,q1, [fi,q2, · · · [fi,qN , fj,q′] · · · ]] = 0.
(33)
The algebra Y∞admits all shifted Yangians (associated to g) as quotients.
Definition 3.2 ([BFN19; Fin+18]). Fix µ ∈P ∨arbitrary. The shifted Yangian Yµ (of g)
is the quotient of Y∞by the relations hi,−⟨µ,αi⟩= 1 and hi,p = 0 for p < −⟨µ, αi⟩.
Remark 3.3. When µ = 0, the algebra Y = Y0 is the usual Yangian of g (with the above
generators and relations recovering its Drinfeld “new presentation”).

4. Representations of shifted Yangians
This section recalls facts about the representation theory of (truncated) shifted Yangians
and defines the important categories for the sections to come. In particular, we recall from
[HZ24] the definition of the category Osh mentioned in Section 1 and study “truncations”
Oλ
sh(R) of this category using results of [Kam+19b]. We also give a new block decomposition
for Osh using ℓ-characters and study the notion of GT-weights3 (as defined in [Kam+19b]).
4.1. The category Osh. There are three notions of weight spaces that one may encounter
while studying modules over shifted Yangians. More precisely, fixing µ ∈P ∨and a repre-
sentation V of Yµ, one can consider either:
(1) simultaneous generalized4 eigenspaces for the finitely-many commuting generators
hi,1−⟨µ,αi⟩(with i ∈I), that is spaces
Vω = {v ∈V | ∃p ∈N such that (hi,1−⟨µ,αi⟩−2⟨α∨
i , ω⟩)pv = 0 for all i ∈I}
defined from an element ω ∈h∗, or
(2) simultaneous generalized eigenspaces for the infinitely-many commuting generators
hi,r (with i ∈I and r > −⟨µ, αi⟩), that is spaces
Vψ = {v ∈V | ∃p ∈N such that (hi,r −ψi,r)pv = 0 for all i ∈I and r > −⟨µ, αi⟩}
defined from a sequence ψ = (ψi,r)i∈I,r>−⟨µ,αi⟩of complex numbers, or, lastly,
3Here “GT” stands for “Gelfand-Tsetlin”.
4The fact that we use generalized eigenspaces here instead of genuine eigenspaces is slightly non-standard
(see upcoming Remark 4.2), but is necessary if we want to use the results of [Kam+19b] on KLRW algebras.

(3) simultaneous generalized eigenspaces for the finitely-many commuting5 elements ai,r

A weight of V is an element ω ∈h∗for which the generalized eigenspace Vω (called weight
space) is non-zero. Analogously, an ℓ-weight of V is a sequence ψ of complex numbers as in
(2) for which the generalized eigenspace Vψ (referred to as an ℓ-weight space) is non-zero.
These notions (with the concept of GT-weight that will be defined in Section 4.5 using the
last type of eigenspaces above) are critical for the study of the representation theory of the
shifted Yangian Yµ. In particular, the first notion allows us to define a BGG category O in
the category Yµ–Mod of all (left) Yµ-modules.
Definition 4.1 ([Kam+24, Section 5]). The category Oµ is the full subcategory of Yµ–Mod
containing the objects V satisfying the conditions:
(O1) V = L
ω∈h∗Vω as vector spaces,
(O2) dim Vω < ∞for all ω ∈h∗, and
(O3) there are ω1, . . . , ωN ∈h∗such that, for all ω ∈h∗,
Vω ̸= 0 =⇒ω ≤ωk for some 1 ≤k ≤N.
Remark 4.2. In [HZ24, Section 3.3], the authors give another definition for the category
Oµ where the weight spaces Vω are replaced with actual eigenspaces, i.e. spaces of the form
{v ∈V | hi,1−⟨µ,αi⟩v = ωiv for all i ∈I}.
The resulting category OHZ
µ
— which is properly contained in the one of Definition 4.1 —
is, as will be detailed in Remark 6.27, too small for our purposes. Nevertheless, as we will
explain below, both Oµ and OHZ
µ
lead to the same collection of simple objects (and hence
to the same Grothendieck group), allowing us to apply directly most of Hernandez–Zhang’s
results to our (slightly more general) context.
By [HZ24, Lemma 2.5] and Definition 4.1, the shifted coproducts ∆µ,ν : Yµ+ν →Yµ ⊗Yν
of Section 3.3 naturally endow the direct sum of abelian categories
Osh = L
µ∈P ∨Oµ
with a tensor product ⊗(defined by pullback) and provide in particular the group K0(Osh)
with a ring structure. Now, as mentioned in Section 1, the primordial goal of this paper is
to show that the ring K0(Osh) (or rather the complexified Grothendieck ring of a “monoidal
skeleton” of Osh) is isomorphic to the coordinate ring of the scheme of bands of [FL25]. To
do this, however, we first need a better understanding of the simple objects in the category
Osh and of the tensor structure on this category.
Remark 4.3. The coproducts ∆µ,ν are not co-associative (i.e. the diagram in property (v)
of Theorem 3.9 does not commute for arbitrary ρ) and it is not known if the category Osh is
monoidal. Nevertheless, we will slightly abuse terminology in this article and use the term
“monoidal” when talking about categorifications of cluster algebras or other related notions.

Fix a simple object V of Oµ. By standard arguments (see, e.g., [Pin24, p.9]), the object V
must be of highest ℓ-weight ψ for some sequence of Laurent series ψ = (ψi(u))i∈I. Moreover,
by Proposition 4.5, this sequence ψ must belong to rµ. On the other hand, [HZ24, Theorem

Theorem 4.7 ([HZ24, Theorem 3.12]). The set {L(ψ)}ψ∈rµ gives a complete collection of
mutually non-isomorphic simple objects for Oµ and OHZ

Remark 4.8 ([HZ24, Example 3.3]). Fix ψ, ξ ∈r and let v and w be the respective highest
ℓ-weight vectors of L(ψ) and L(ξ). Then, by [HZ24, Lemma 2.5], for all i ∈I,

so that v⊗w generates a submodule of highest ℓ-weight ψξ in the tensor product L(ψ)⊗L(ξ).
In particular, the simple module L(ψξ) is necessarily a composition factor of L(ψ) ⊗L(ξ).

We end this subsection by giving a block decomposition for (the finite-length subcategory
of) Osh. While new in this generality, the decomposition we give is reminiscent of the one
obtained using elliptic characters in [EM03; CM05] for the category C of finite-dimensional
type I representations of unshifted quantum affine algebras. This decomposition is moreover
closely related to the map awt : B →B/Γ of Section 2.2 and the truncations of Section 3.2
(see Section 5.5 for this last point). We start by showing the result below.
Theorem 4.21. Fix a coweight µ of g with ψ, ξ ∈rµ. Suppose that there exists a non-trivial
extension of L(ψ) by L(ξ). Then either ψ ⪯ξ or ξ ⪯ψ.
Proof. Take a non-trivial extension M of L(ψ) by L(ξ) and suppose ψ ̸⪯ξ. Corollary 4.16
gives dim L(ξ)ψm = 0 for all m ∈A+. In particular, as χℓ(M) = χℓ(L(ψ)) + χℓ(L(ξ)), by
this same corollary,
(47)
dim Mψ = dim L(ψ)ψ = 1 and dim MψAi,a = dim L(ψ)ψAi,a = 0
for all i ∈I and a ∈C. Choose v ∈Mψ. By (47) and Theorem 4.15, hj(u)v = ψj(u)v and
ej(u)v = 0 for every j ∈I. Also, v does not belong to the submodule L ⊆M isomorphic to
L(ξ) and it follows (from non-triviality of the extension M) that M = Yµ · v is a module of
highest ℓ-weight ψ. In particular, ξ ⪯ψ by Corollary 4.16. This ends the proof.
□
We can now define the aforementioned “blocks” of Osh. Recall that A denotes the group
of Laurent monomials in the A’s and fix µ ∈P ∨. Let also πµ : rµ ↠(r/A)µ be the canonical
projection and take τ ∈(r/A)µ. We let τOµ be the Serre subcategory of Oµ generated by
the simple objects L(ψ) with highest ℓ-weight ψ ∈rµ satisfying πµ(ψ) = τ.
We call τOµ a “block” of Oµ because of the following result.

Proof. The first statement follows from Theorem 4.21 by the usual argument involving long
exact sequences and double induction (on the lengths of V1 and V2). The second statement

Remark 4.23. The block decomposition of Theorem 4.22 is intrinsically related to the map
awt : B ↠B/Γ of Section 2.2. Indeed, identify r with the group BC of Section 2.4 using the
isomorphism yi,a 7→Ψi,a. Then the subgroup A ⊆r corresponds to the weight-0 component
(ΓC)0 of ΓC ⊆BC and the map
πµ : rµ →rµ/A,
underlying the decomposition of Theorem 4.22, is naturally identified with the map
(BC)µ →(BC)µ/(ΓC)0
obtained from awt (or its natural extension to BC) by restricting to the weight-µ component.
One can hence think of Theorem 4.22 as giving a block decomposition with respect to awt.

5. Results on tensor products
This section proves various new results about tensor products in the category Osh of the
precedent section. More precisely, we consider in Section 5.1 distinguished anti-involutions
of shifted Yangians and use them to construct a canonical contravariant autofunctor of Osh
that we prove is compatible with tensor products. We then use this functor and associators
of [Zha24] to extend results of [HZ24], producing along the way normalized R-matrices for
many pairs of simple objects of Osh, and deduce that tensor products of simple modules in
Osh are generically irreducible. Finally, we show in Section 5.4 that the coproducts ∆µ,ν of
Section 3.3 give coproducts ∆λ1,λ2
µ1,µ2(R1, R2) : Y λ1+λ2
µ1+µ2 (R1 ∪R2) →Y λ1
µ1 (R1) ⊗Y λ2
µ2 (R2) for
truncations. Byproducts of this construction are: (1) another criterion equivalent to those
of Theorem 2.12 and (2) a link between the blocks of Theorem 4.22 and truncations.

Remark 5.3. The contravariance and exactness of tr with the above two results imply that
the tensor products L(ψ1)⊗L(ψ2) and L(ψ2)⊗L(ψ1) ∼= tr(L(ψ1)⊗L(ψ2)) have reciprocal
structures (i.e. they share the same composition factors, but these factors are organized in
reciprocal ways in the two, typically non-isomorphic, tensor products). This gives another
proof (simpler than the one using Theorem 4.13) that K0(Osh) is commutative (and one can
even see the existence of the functor tr as categorifying this commutativity statement).
5.2. R-matrices in Osh. In [HZ24], the authors define morphisms RV,W : V ⊗W →W ⊗V ,
that they call R-matrices, for particular tensor products of modules in Osh. More precisely,
Hernandez and Zhang first show the following result:
Theorem 5.4 ([HZ24, Theorem 4.8]). Let V, W be objects of Osh. Then the tensor product
V ⊗W is of highest ℓ-weight if either
(i) V is a positive prefundamental representation and W is of highest ℓ-weight, or
(ii) V is of highest ℓ-weight and W is a negative prefundamental representation.
Similarly, V ⊗W is a co-highest ℓ-weight module if either
(iii) V is of co-highest ℓ-weight and W is a positive prefundamental representation, or
(iv) V is a negative prefundamental representation and W is of highest ℓ-weight.
Here, a Yµ-module U of co-highest ℓ-weight ψ is a representation such that
(1) U is top-graded, that is dim Uwt(ψ) = 1 with Uω = 0 unless ω ≤wt(ψ), and
(2) Uψ is contained in every non-zero submodule of U.
Clearly, a Yµ-module U is of highest ℓ-weight ψ if and only if its dual tr(U) is of co-highest
ℓ-weight ψ. Furthermore, in this situation,
top(U) ∼= L(ψ) ∼= soc(tr(U))
and there is, up to scalar, a unique morphism U →tr(U). An easy corollary of this is:
Corollary 5.5 ([HZ24, Theorem 5.2]). Fix V, W simple objects in Osh with highest ℓ-weight
vectors v ∈V and w ∈W. Suppose that either
(i) V is a positive prefundamental representation, or
(ii) W is a negative prefundamental representation.
Then there is a unique morphism RV,W : V ⊗W →W ⊗V that sends v ⊗w to w ⊗v.

by Lemma 5.1 and Theorem 5.2 so that the above discussion ends the proof.
□

Following [HZ24], we call R-matrices the maps defined in the above corollary. We expect
these maps to facilitate, in the future, the proof of numerous results about the category Osh
(like the maps from Corollary 5.5 did in [HZ24]) and thus think of Corollary 5.13 as a result
of independent interest from the principal results of this article. In particular, as mentioned
in Section 1, we expect our R-matrices to be part of a collection of renormalized R-matrices
(in the spirit of Kashiwara–Kim–Oh–Park, see, e.g., [Kas+24]) which could potentially play
a key role in the proof that Osh is, as conjectured in [GHL24], a monoidal categorification of
a cluster algebra. We also expect that our R-matrices could help define monoidal Jantzen
filtrations (as introduced in [FH26]) for the setting of modules over shifted Yangians. This
would then give rise to a canonical deformation Kt of the Grothendieck ring K0(Osh) (see
[Pag26] for a related deformation coming from the perspective of cluster algebras, and the
upcoming Remark 6.31 for yet another deformation coming from parity KLRW-algebras).
We end this subsection by giving an explicit consequence of the above results, namely an
extension to Osh of (a weak version of) the main results of [Her10; Her19] (which can them-
selves be seen as the culmination of results shown in [CP91b; FM01; Cha02; HL10])15. We
expect that this extension will be helpful for the proof that Osh is a monoidal categorifica-
tion of cluster algebra, and will talk briefly about stronger versions of it after we introduce
in Section 12 the automorphism D mentioned in Remark 5.10.
We will assume the (widely believed) conjecture below, for which substantial evidence was
recently communicated to us by H. Zhang.
Conjecture 5.14. Take V1, V2, V3 in Osh. Then there exists an isomorphism
V1 ⊗(V2 ⊗V3) ∼= (V1 ⊗V2) ⊗V3.
Thus, k-fold tensor products in Osh are independent of the choice of parenthesization, up to
(possibly non-canonical) isomorphism.
Corollary 5.15. For 1 ≤r ≤k, fix Pr, Qr ∈r polynomial and let Vr = L( Pr
Qr ). Suppose
that Conjecture 5.14 holds. Then (omitting parentheses)

(ii) V1 ⊗· · · ⊗Vk is of co-highest ℓ-weight if Vr ⊗L(Ps) is simple for 1 ≤s < r ≤k.
In particular,
(iii) V1 ⊗· · · ⊗Vk is simple if Vr ⊗L(Ps) is simple for all 1 ≤r, s ≤k with r ̸= s.

5.3. Generic simplicity of products. Write r(0) for the group of integral ℓ-weights, that
is the image of the integral crystal B ⊆BC via the correspondence BC ∼= r of Remark 4.23.
This group gives rise to a monoidal full subcategory Osh(0) of Osh via the next definition:
Definition 5.16. The category Osh(0) is defined as the Serre subcategory of Osh generated
by the simple objects with highest ℓ-weights in r(0).
Clearly, one could also define a monoidal Serre subcategory O ⊆Osh(0) by imposing the
stronger condition that all the ℓ-weights of all the objects of the subcategory lie inside r(0).
However, as we will show below, this category O actually coincides with Osh(0).

Interlude
Starting next section, we make some significant notational changes.
• We replace the Lie algebra g by its Langlands dual g∨(and consider shifted Yangians
for g∨). This replaces all weights by coweights, all roots by coroots, and vice-versa.
• We replace the category Osh by the full subcategory of finite-length objects in the
integral category Osh(0) of Definition 5.16. Similarly, we replace r by the group r(0)
of integral ℓ-weights, which is by definition isomorphic to the monomial crystal B,
and replace A by A ∩r(0). Recall that Osh(0) is monoidal by Corollary 5.20 (since
we work under the assumption that Conjecture 5.14 holds).
• All sets of parameters considered will be integral. The collection of (integral) sets
of parameters of level λ ∈P will be denoted Zλ.
• We replace the set of GT-weights Λν = Q
i∈I Cmi/Σmi (where ν = P
i∈I miαi ∈Q+)
by its integral counterpart, i.e. by the subset
{S = (Si)i∈I ∈Λν | Si ⊆i + 2Z for each i ∈I}.


## Page 55


TRUNCATED SHIFTED YANGIANS AND THE BI-INFINITE BOTT-SAMELSON VARIETY
55
In particular, since the category Osh is (now) of finite-length, a natural Z-basis for the ring
K0(Osh) is given by the set of classes of simple objects, i.e. by the [L(ψ)]’s with ψ ∈r ∼= B.
In addition, by Theorem 5.23, a simple object in the general (i.e. non-integral) version of the
category Osh of the previous pages can always be factorized as a tensor product of spectral
shifts of simple objects with integral highest ℓ-weights. We hence do not lose any generality
in considering the Grothendieck ring of our integral category Osh instead of that of (the full
subcategory of finite-length objects in) the non-integral version of this category.
6. KLR algebras and their variants
We briefly recall the framework of KLR algebras due independently to [KL09; KL11] and
[Rou08]. These diagrammatic algebras provide a categorification of the negative-half Uq(n−)
of the quantum group Uq(g) (for q a formal parameter associated to a grading functor) and
their cyclotomic quotients categorify irreducible representations of g by [KK12] or [Web17].
We will not give proper definitions here and refer the reader to the above-mentioned papers
for a detailed introduction. Throughout, we work with non-graded C-algebras.
6.1. KLR algebras. For ν ∈Q+, let Rν be the KLR algebra for g of height ν. This algebra

6.3. KLRW algebras. In [Web17], diagrammatic algebras generalizing the classical KLR
algebras were introduced to categorify tensor products of irreducible representations. They
depend on a list of dominant weights

6.7. Relation with truncated shifted Yangians. We now study an equivalence of cat-
egories which relates modules over parity KLRW algebras and truncated shifted Yangians.

Lemma 7.3. Fix finite-dimensional G-representations V and W. Then every B-equivariant

8. G-action and multiplication
The goal of this section is to use the actions of G and A on the algebra KC(Osh) to show
new results on its multiplication. The main byproducts of this pursuit are various inclusions
of the coordinate ring of the base affine space N−\\G in K0(Osh) and proofs of conjectures
of Frenkel–Hernandez and Geiss–Hernandez–Leclerc on extended QQ-systems (cf. Section
1). We start by relating distinct chamber modules through the G-action.

8.2. Height functions and the coordinate ring of the base affine space. We now
take a slight geometric tangent in preparation for the upcoming Sections 9–10.
We adopt the convention that geometric objects are endowed with right-actions and that
their ring of functions are equipped with the induced left-actions (and vice-versa). We start
by clarifying the impact of these conventions.

Remark 8.18. We recall that the results shown here for representations of shifted Yangians
imply the analogous results for representations of shifted quantum affine algebras (by [VV25,
Corollary 1.2.1]). Also, one can use the above results in the setting of Borel quantum affine
algebras using recent results [HN26, Section 4].
As explained in Section 1, the Q-variables (95) and the extended QQ-system (97) respec-
tively correspond to initial cluster variables and mutation relations for the cluster algebra
studied in [GHL24]. Thus Theorem 8.13 and Theorem 8.16 can be seen as first steps toward
proving [GHL24, Conjecture 9.16] (which, in essence, states that the category Osh gives a
monoidal categorification of the above cluster algebra).

8.5. Chamber modules are real prime simple modules. Recall that a simple object V
of Osh is said to be real if V ⊗2 is simple, and prime if, for every factorization V ∼= V1 ⊗V2,
either V1 or V2 is the trivial representation L(1). In this section, we show that all chamber
modules are real and prime.
Fix λ ∈P+, R ∈Zλ and w ∈W. Since the wλ-weight space of B(λ, R) ⊆N
i∈I B(ϖi)⊗λi
contains one element, Theorem 4.25 shows that Oλ
wλ(R) has a unique simple object Lwλ,R
(up to isomorphism). We call such a module Lwλ,R extremal. Clearly, chamber modules are
extremal modules associated to fundamental weights.

Remark 8.29. The initial cluster variables of the cluster algebra of [GHL24] all correspond

definition of monoidal categorification of cluster algebra given in21 [HL10, Definition 2.1].
9. Background on Bott–Samelson varieties
Throughout Sections 9 and 10, all schemes and pro-varieties are over C. Our convention,
as in Section 8.2, is that the geometric objects we study are equipped with right G-actions,
and their rings of functions (or more generally, spaces of sections of equivariant line bun-
dles) are equipped with the induced left G-action. Hence we will adopt the convention of
considering V (λ)∗as a right G-module. Moreover, v∗
wλ will denote the vector of weight wλ
in the right module V (λ)∗, normalized so that its pairing with vwλ is 1.
9.1. Generalized flag varieties. Given a subset J ⊆I, let FlJ be the G-orbit of ([v∗
ϖi])i∈J
in Q
i∈J P(V (ϖi)∗). The stabilizer of ([v∗
ϖi])i∈J is the parabolic subgroup P −
¯J [Pro07, Chap-
ter 10.6.2] where ¯J = I\J, so FlJ is isomorphic to the generalized partial flag variety P −
¯J \G.
Moreover, since P −
¯J \G is complete, FlJ is a closed subscheme of Q
i∈J P(V (ϖi)∗). This is
typically called the Pl¨ucker embedding of P −
¯J \G. We will denote the full flag variety FlI by
Fl and we will denote the generalized partial flag variety Fl{i} corresponding to a maximal
parabolic by Gr(i) (as these are the usual Grassmannians in type A).

Given two points x, y of Gr(i), Gr(j) respectively, we say that x is incident to y, denoted
x ∼y, if they satisfy the condition
x ⊗y ∈P(V (ϖi + ϖj)∗) ⊆P(V (ϖi)∗⊗V (ϖj)∗)
(this definition applies to R-points for every C-algebra R, and in fact defines a closed sub-
scheme of Gr(i) × Gr(j)). Then Theorem 9.1 is equivalent to saying that FlJ parametrizes
tuples (xi)i∈J ∈Q
i∈J Gr(i) satisfying the incidence equations xi ∼xj for all i ̸= j in J.
Example 9.2. If G = SLn, then Gr(i) is the Grassmannian of i-dimensional subspaces
of Cn, and the incidence conditions correspond simply to inclusion of subspaces. Then Fl
precisely parametrizes tuples of subspaces (W1, . . . , Wn−1) of Cn with dim Wi = i, such
that Wi is contained in Wj for every i < j.

the multi-homogeneous coordinate ring, Corollary 9.4 is of a somewhat different flavor. It
does not mean that the defining ideal of the multi-homogeneous coordinate ring is generated
by the kernels of Sym2 V (ϖi) ↠V (2ϖi) and of V (ϖi)⊗V (ϖj) ↠V (ϖi+ϖj) for i adjacent
to j (in fact, this must be false simply for degree reasons).
9.2. Bott–Samelson and free Bott–Samelson varieties. In this section, we recall im-
portant facts about Bott–Samelson varieties. We will use a realization of Bott–Samelson
varieties as subschemes of products of Gr(i)’s defined by incidence relations. This descrip-
tion (at a set-theoretic rather than scheme-theoretic level) first appears in [Mag98b] to the
best of our knowledge.
It will be convenient for us to think of Bott–Samelson varieties as associated primarily
to combinatorial gadgets introduced by Viennot called heaps [Vie86], which are in bijection
with words in the alphabet I up to commutation relations.
Definition 9.6. A heap is a finite partially ordered set (H, ≤) along with a map c : H →I
satisfying
(1) For i, j ∈I that are either adjacent or equal, c−1({i, j}) ⊆H is totally ordered.
(2) For every covering relation h ⋖h′ in H, c(h) and c(h′) are either adjacent or equal.


## Page 86


86
J. KAMNITZER, A. LABELLE, A. LEROUX-LAPIERRE, T. PINET, AND A. WEEKES
Given a heap (H, ≤, c), we will denote by Hi the subset c−1(i) ⊆H, so that H = F
i∈I Hi.
We say that a heap H is alternating if Hi and Hj are always perfectly interlaced, i.e. for
every edge i ∼j of the Dynkin diagram and every h, h′ ∈Hi with h < h′, there exists
h′′ ∈Hj such that h < h′′ < h′. All the heaps that we will consider later in Section 10 will
be alternating.
Definition 9.7. Given two heaps (H1, ≤1, c1) and (H2, ≤2, c2), the product H1 ⊙H2 is the
heap with underlying set H1 ⊔H2, whose map to I extends c1 : H1 →I and c2 : H2 →I,
and whose partial order is defined as the transitive closure of the relation defined by h ≤h′
if either
• h, h′ ∈H1 and h ≤1 h′, or
• h, h′ ∈H2 and h ≤2 h′, or
• h ∈H1, h′ ∈H2 and c1(h), c2(h′) are either adjacent or equal.
Given a word i = (i1, . . . , ir) ∈Ir, let
H(i) = H(i1) ⊙· · · ⊙H(ir),
where H(i) is the heap with a unique element ∗and c(∗) = i.
Lemma 9.8. [Vie86, Lemmas 3.2 and 3.3] The map i 7→H(i) defines a bijection between
words in I up to commutation relations (i.e. the exchange of ik and ik+1 in i if ik ̸∼ik+1)
and heaps (up to isomorphism). Also, given a heap H, the set of words i such that H ∼= H(i)
is in bijection with linear extensions of H, i.e. enumerations h1, . . . , hr of the elements of
H such that hk ≤hℓimplies k ≤ℓ, where a linear extension h1, . . . , hr corresponds to the
word (c(h1), . . . , c(hr)).
One way to represent heaps visually is via the “abacus model” [KR10; Dra+24], where
the elements on H are represented by beads on an abacus with rods indexed by I, where
the rods are placed in such a way that the beads on two adjacent rods are forced to interlace
(i.e. the distance between two adjacent rods is smaller than the diameter of the beads).
Any pairs of beads touching each other correspond to a covering relation in the poset H.
Given a word i = (i1, . . . , ir), the corresponding heap H(i) is obtained by dropping a bead
on rods i1, then one on rod i2, and so on until the last bead on rod ir. Figure 2, for
example, shows a heap of type A3 using this representation.
Figure 2. The heap H(2, 1, 3, 2, 1, 2, 3) = H(2, 3, 1, 2, 1, 2, 3). This heap is
not alternating, as there is no bead on the 3rd rod between the highest beads
of the 2nd rod (but adding a bead there would yield an alternating heap).

We now compute the character of B(λ, R) via the method given in [Gib21]. This method
involves auxiliary truncations B(λ, R, J) ⊆B(λ, R) depending on an upward-closed set
J ⊆I ×2 Z, consisting of the monomials in B(λ, R) whose support lie in J.
Define a
sequence of triples (λ0, R0, J0), . . . , (λr, Rr, Jr) by the following rules:
• λr = 0, Rr = ∅, and Jr is the upward-closed set with boundary ξ′, i.e.
Jr = {(i, a) ∈I ×2 Z : a ≥ξ′
i}.
• For k ≤r, λk−1 = λk + mik,bk+1ϖik, Rk−1 is obtained from Rk by adjoining bk + 1
to Rik with multiplicity mik,bk+1, and Jk = Jk+1 ⊔{(ik, bk −1)}.
Note that (λ0, R0) = (λ, R) and Rk is supported in Jk for all k. By [Gib21, Theorem 5.9],
ch B(λr, Rr, Jr) = 1
and
ch B(λk−1, Rk−1, Jk−1) = Λik(emik,bk+1ϖik ch B(λk, Rk, Jk))
for k ≤r. Therefore, we have
ch B(λ0, R0, J0) = Λi1(emi1,b1+1ϖi1Λi2(emi2,b2+1ϖi2 · · · Λir(emir,br+1ϖir ) · · · )).
Moreover, by [Gib21, Corollary 5.14], we have
ch B(λ, R) = ch B(λ0, R0) = Λw0(ch B(λ0, R0, J0)).
Comparing with (117) yields the desired equality of characters.
□
We are now in a position to fulfil a promise made in the proof of Theorem 2.15.
Corollary 10.27. Fix λ ∈P+ together with a height function ξ. Let R ∈Zλ be the set of
parameters given by ξ. Then, R is maximally singular.
Proof. As Zξ,ξ ∼= Z◦
ξ,ξ ∼= B−\G, the representation V (λ, R) coincides with the representa-
tion V (λ) by the Borel-Weil Theorem. Since the previous theorem shows that V (λ, R) has
the same character as B(λ, R), the statement follows.
□
Remark 10.28. Take G = SLm . In [Gib21, Theorem 6.23], Gibson shows that B(λ, R) is
the crystal of a generalized Schur module. On the other hand, in [Mag98a], Magyar gives
a Borel-Weil construction for the generalized Schur modules. Thus, Theorem 10.26 can be
regarded as a generalization of Magyar’s result from type A to any simply-laced group.

a good understanding of the space of global sections of globally generated line bundles on

9.14) or its product monomial crystal interpretation (Theorem 10.26). In this section, we
use our isomorphism Ωand the combinatorics of product monomial crystals to deduce a
description of H0(Z∞, Lm) for any Laurent monomial m ∈B.

Remark 11.12. Given height functions ξ ≤ξ′, Theorem 11.9 implies that, for every line

boundary divisors {Di,b | (i, b) ∈H(ξ, ξ′)}. From this perspective, it is natural to ask if the
corresponding statement holds for an arbitrary free Bott–Samelson variety ZH (and/or for
the non-free version ZH). Indeed, this statement holds for ZH whenever H is alternating
(because of Remark 10.2 and Theorem 11.9), but it is reasonable to hope that a more direct
geometric proof exists and would work for any H.

The results of this section can be summarized as follows:
Corollary 11.21. When g = sln, the algebra KC(Osh) is generated by the classes of cham-
ber modules associated to ϖ1, with only the relations coming from the BGG resolution.
We find it remarkable that all the relations in K0(Osh) can in this case be deduced from
a single standard fact about the representation theory of sln.
12. Extension of Hernandez–Leclerc’s duality
This section defines an algebra involution D of K0(Osh) which extends the rational ana-
logue of the isomorphism given in [HL16, Section 5.3] for quantum affine Borel algebras and

in [Her23, Theorem 8.7] for shifted quantum affine algebras. We show that the involution D
sends classes of simple modules to classes of simple modules using the general framework of
cobased modules (see Definition 12.4) with Lusztig’s work [Lus93] on canonical bases. This
answers (the obvious counterpart of) a question asked in [Pin24, Appendix A].

We will need Lusztig’s formalism of based modules [Lus93, Section 27.1.2], or, more pre-
cisely the dual formalism (which we formulate in the same spirit as Khovanov [Kho97]).
Definition 12.4. A pair (V, B) of a left U-module and a basis B ⊆V is a cobased module if
the pair (V, B∨), with B∨the basis dual to B (for the inner product (., .)V given after (133)),
is a based module in the sense of [Lus93, Section 27.1.2].
In particular, if (V, B) is a cobased module, then B is a weight basis of V . Moreover, in
this case, one can define a C-linear map σB : V →V given (for f(q) ∈C(q) and b ∈B) by
σB(f(q)b) = f(q−1)b
and show that this map satisfies
(136)
σB(av) = σ(a)σB(v)
for all a ∈U and v ∈V .
Theorem 12.5 ([Lus93, Section 27.4.1]). For λ ∈P+, (V (λ), B(λ)) is a cobased module.

A direct consequence of the above is the following corollary:
Corollary 12.18. The involution D of KC(Osh) induces an algebra involution of K0(Osh).

We now investigate the multiplicativity of d. We first easily deduce:
Lemma 12.20. Fix ψ1, ψ2 ∈r for which the tensor product L(ψ1)⊗L(ψ2) is simple. Then
d(ψ1ψ2) = d(ψ1)d(ψ2).
Proof. Let ψ = ψ1ψ2. Then L(ψ1)⊗L(ψ2) ∼= L(ψ) and, since D is an algebra isomorphism,
[L(d(ψ)] = D[L(ψ)] = D([L(ψ1)][L(ψ2)]) = D([L(ψ1)])D([L(ψ2)]) = [L(d(ψ1)][L(d(ψ2))].
Hence L(d(ψ1)) ⊗L(d(ψ2)) ∼= L(d(ψ)), and thus d(ψ1)d(ψ2) = d(ψ).
□
In particular, d(ψ2) = d(ψ)2 if ψ ∈r is the highest ℓ-weight of a real simple object of Osh
(see Theorem 8.28). On the other extreme, the next result shows that d is also multiplicative
on ℓ-weights that lie in generic product monomial crystals.

Appendix A. Pro-varieties
The algebro-geometric objects that we consider in Section 10 are inverse limits of finite-
dimensional algebraic varieties. Such limits do not always exist in the category of schemes,
so we need to consider them as formal inverse limits in the pro-completion of the category
of varieties. In this appendix, we set up some basic definitions regarding these pro-varieties
as well as coherent sheaves on them.
Let k be a field. By a variety over k, we mean a scheme of finite type over k. Let Vark
denote the category of varieties over k.
Definition A.1. The category of pro-varieties over k is the pro-completion Pro(Vark) of
the category of k-varieties. Specifically, the objects are formal inverse limits
lim
←

There is a fully faithful embedding of Vark into Pro(Vark) given by considering trivial
diagrams with a single object, and every object X of Vark is cocompact in Pro(Vark), mean-
ing that Hom(−, X) : Pro(Vark)op →Set sends cofiltered limits in Pro(Vark) to filtered
colimits in Set. Moreover, the pro-completion satisfies the following universal property: for
every category C that admits cofiltered limits and every fully faithful functor F : Vark →C
that lands in cocompact objects, there is a unique (up to unique isomorphism) extension of
F to a cofiltered limit-preserving functor F ′ : Pro(Vark) →C. Specifically, F ′ maps a for-
mal inverse limit lim
←Xi to the inverse limit in C of the diagram (F(Xi))i∈I.

• objects of Coh(X) are all of the form π∗

In fact, Coh is a 2-functor from Pro(Vark)op to the bicategory of categories, meaning that
a morphism f : X →Y of pro-varieties always induces a pullback f∗: Coh(Y ) →Coh(X),
compatibly with composition of morphisms. Thus, isomorphisms of pro-varieties give equiv-
alences of categories of coherent sheaves.

In other words, every global section is defined at some finite level, and two global sections

is functorial, in the sense that a morphism F →G of coherent sheaves on X induces a map
H0(X, F) →H0(X, G) and that a morphism f : X →Y of pro-varieties induces a map
H0(Y, F) →H0(X, f∗F) for every F ∈Coh(Y ), compatibly with compositions.
Remark A.4. Let Schk be the category of quasi-compact quasi-separated schemes over k.
By [Sta25, Tag 01YX], Schk admits limits of cofiltered diagrams with affine transition mor-
phisms27. Moreover, if X is a variety, then Hom(−, X) sends such limits to colimits in Set
by [Sta25, Tag 01ZC].
If we let Pro(Vark)aff. be the full subcategory of Pro(Vark) consisting of the pro-varieties
obtained as the limit of a diagram with affine transition morphisms, it follows that we have
a fully faithful embedding Pro(Vark)aff. ,→Schk sending a formal inverse limit lim
←Xi to the
actual inverse limit in Schk. We can therefore think of such pro-varieties as actual schemes.
Furthermore, by [Sta25, Tag 01ZR, Tag 0B8W, Tag 01Z0], our notions of coherent sheaves,
line bundles and global sections agree, in this situation, with the usual notions for schemes.
Without the assumption on affine transition morphisms, however, pro-varieties generally
cannot be regarded as schemes. See for example [Sta25, Tag 078E], which explains why the
product of infinitely many copies of P1 (which is the same as our bi-infinite Bott–Samelson
variety in type A1) does not exist in the category of schemes.
Appendix B. Inflations
Inflations were introduced in [Pin25] as distinguished preimages for canonical restriction
functors arising in the study of representations of shifted quantum affine algebras. We define
their rational analogue, for representations of shifted Yangians, here and use truncations to
prove counterparts of conjectures and results given in [Pin25].
27The reference we give assumes that the indexing category I is (the dual of) a directed set rather than a
cofiltered category. This restriction does not come with any loss of generality, thanks to [Sta25, Tag 0032].

Definition B.2. Fix an object W in Oν(gJ) with a coweight µ ∈P ∨such that resJ(µ) = ν.
Then, a J-inflation of W of coweight µ to g (or just inflation of W to g if µ and J are clear
from the context) is an object V of Oµ satisfying the conditions
(i) resµ

As explained in [Pin25, Section 4.2], the notion of inflation allows one to reduce problems
in Osh to analogous (typically easier) problems in the category Osh(gJ) = L
ν∈P ∨
J Oν(gJ).
In particular, inflations are compatible with the notion of real and prime modules, and are
expected to have natural applications in the setting of monoidal categorifications of cluster
algebras. A natural (and very basic) question about these is however:
Question B.3. Do all simple modules in Osh(gJ) admit an inflation to g?
This question was answered positively for shifted quantum affine algebras of type A–B–G
(or for finite-dimensional irreducible modules of arbitrary shifted quantum affine algebras)
in [Pin25, Corollary 3.29 and Theorem 3.40] using:
(1) the compatibility between inflations and fusion/tensor products with
(2) a technical study of ℓ-characters of negative prefundamental representations.
The purpose of this section is to answer Question B.3 in full generality, therefore proving the
rational analogue of [Pin25, Conjecture 1.4]. We use for this a different approach from the
one used in [Pin25], and rely on notable equalities involving truncations for shifted Yangians
of the form Yµ and Yν(gJ) (as in [Kam+18, Section 5.2]). We think that our approach could
also be adapted to the context of shifted quantum affine algebras, but leave this adaptation
for further work (see [Pin25, Section 4.1] for details).

B.3. A notable example. It is natural to ask if all inflations can be constructed as above
(i.e. using equalities of truncations as in Lemma B.4 and (154)). This is unfortunately
not the case. Indeed, choose i ∈I and let J = {i} ⊆I. Then gJ ∼= sl2 and one can
understand the self-extension V of Example 5.42 as a Y0(gJ)-module.
